One Customer, End to End

A real fill, traced through the data and the two models

pipeline v1 draws the reasoning. pipeline v2 draws the tables. This page is the evidence — ten real customers carried from the raw rows we trained on, through the prediction we wrote down, to what they actually did afterwards.

It is written to be audited, not admired. Every number is reproducible from the repo, and the places where the evidence is thin say so.

How to read this page

  1. The headline — one chart, every customer, predicted against actual.
  2. The ten customers — a tab each, with the raw data, the prediction and the outcome collapsed inside. Open what you want to interrogate.
  3. The mechanism — how a single fill becomes an offer, step by step.
  4. The check that carries weight — 583,583 scored fills, not ten.
  5. What this cannot show — the gap no amount of data here closes.

Figures come from the 24-store pilot build (config.PILOT_STORES). The columns and the code are real; margins are assumed, because the extract has no cost column.

1 · The headline

Seven of the ten customers came back after the cutoff. For each one, what we predicted for their category against what they actually did:

Read the gap, not the dots. A short bar is a good prediction. The soda regular at 70 fills is a ring — predicted and actual land on the same point, 94.2% against 94.3%. The cigarette regular’s 22-point gap is on 8 fills, which is noise, and the chart says so by printing the count.

2 · The ten customers, end to end

Each tab is one real shopper: the raw rows we trained on, the prediction in customer_scores.parquet, what actually happened, and what ALS believed against what they bought. Sections start collapsed.

Tabs read archetype · held-out fills. Two shoppers per archetype — the one personas.py picks for being most typical before the cutoff, and one that also came back.

One customer cannot validate a probability. personas.py selects for being typical and never asks whether the shopper reappears — so four of its five have no held-out record, and a fifth has two fills. That is why every tab also carries the whole cohort matching that archetype, from 433 to 105,866 held-out fills.

  • never in · 223
  • soda · 2
  • cigs · 0
  • salty · 0
  • beer · 0
  • never in · 156
  • soda · 70
  • cigs · 8
  • salty · 9
  • beer · 19

never bought — a57e922fe549…. 1,069 fills before the cutoff, 223 after it, so this one can be checked against its own outcome.

3 · The check that carries weight

Sections 1 and 2 are illustrations. This is the section that decides whether any of it can be used, and it is worth saying why.

An offer is chosen by multiplying a probability by dollars. That makes ranking accuracy almost irrelevant and scale accuracy everything: a model that sorts customers perfectly but reports 0.34 where the truth is 0.20 will rank the right person first and then price the offer 70% too high. AUC cannot see that error at all — it is invariant to any monotone rescaling of the scores. Calibration is the only check that looks at it.

Ten customers cannot answer that. Every scored row can. Taking all 583,583 held-out fills, sorting them by predicted P(buys a beverage) and cutting into ten equal buckets:

Decile Predicted Actual Gap
1 (lowest) 0.0001 0.0005 +0.0004
3 0.0119 0.0157 +0.0038
5 0.0385 0.0395 +0.0009
7 0.0678 0.0671 −0.0007
9 0.1400 0.1407 +0.0008
10 (highest) 0.3430 0.3287 −0.0144

The middle of the distribution is calibrated to within a tenth of a point. The top decile is the one to watch: at a predicted 34.3% the observed rate is 32.9%, so the model is mildly over-confident exactly where it is most willing to spend money. That is the right direction to be wrong in for a sceptic and the wrong direction for a budget — an offer priced off the top decile is buying about 4% less attach than the arithmetic assumes.

The bottom three deciles run slightly under-confident, which is the signature of isotonic calibration flattening at the floor.

What we can conclude from this

The probabilities can be used as probabilities. Through the middle of the distribution predicted and observed agree to within a tenth of a point, so multiplying them by a margin is legitimate arithmetic rather than a rescaling of a score. That is the permission this whole pipeline needs.

Discount any budget built off the top decile by about 4%. Predicted 34.3% against an observed 32.9% is a 1.4-point gap — small absolutely, but it sits precisely where we are most willing to spend, so it is the error that costs money. It is also the safe direction to be wrong in: we overestimate the value of the offers we are keenest on, rather than missing good ones.

The under-confidence at the bottom is harmless. Those are customers we would not offer to anyway; being slightly pessimistic about someone we were going to show nothing to changes no decision.

What it does not let us conclude

Calibration says our prediction of what they do anyway is accurate. It says nothing about what an offer changes — p₁ appears nowhere in it. A perfectly calibrated model of the status quo is still no evidence that a discount moves anybody, which is why the holdout remains the ask.

And stage two: did it name the item they bought?

Calibration says nothing about the SKU. The direct test, on the 25,894 held-out fills where ALS ran and the customer bought inside the offered category:

k SKU Affinity Store best seller Lift
1 9.1% 3.2% 2.8×
5 24.2% 11.4% 2.1×
10 34.3% 16.9% 2.0×

k = 1 is the row that matters, because the screen shows one offer. Across all evaluable fills — personalised and fallback together — we name the exact item at 6.2% against the best seller’s 2.9%. Quoting hit@10 and stopping there would overstate what the product does: ten guesses is not the product, one is.

Both rankings scored on the same rows. Scoring ALS rows against fallback rows would compare two different populations — the ALS path only exists for shoppers with history, who buy more anyway — and would flatter the model for reasons that have nothing to do with it.

The conclusion is narrower than it looks. ALS doubles hit@10 where it runs, and it runs on 31.6% of fills. Weight the two paths together and the personalised tier moves the overall hit rate from 16.9% to about 22% — real, and smaller than the 2× headline. The practical consequence: the quality of the best-seller fallback matters more to the product than the quality of ALS, because it answers two fills in three.

On fallback rows the two rankings agree 100.000% of the time. They are the same ranking by construction, so that is the control that says the harness works. validate_sku.py prints the breakdown by category.

How one fill becomes an offer

Sections 1–3 are the evidence. These are the mechanism: a single customer carried through the pipeline, one step at a time, with the numbers the models actually produced at each step.

And the second item: the one bet we cannot settle

The pair offer had never been scored. It has now, on the 43,850 held-out fills where the customer bought the anchor category and carried a second-item offer:

Landed
Our pair offer 10.8%
Most popular second category 14.0%

We are 0.78× the naive baseline, and that is the design working. The mechanism is visible when you split by what we offered:

We offered Fills They bought it They bought Salty Snacks
Salty Snacks 37,800 11.9% 11.9%
Alternative Snacks 5,233 4.5% 26.0%

On the fills where headroom steers us away from Salty Snacks — because this shopper already buys it constantly — they go on to buy Salty Snacks 26% of the time, and the thing we offered instead 4.5%.

That trade is the entire bet of the second-item idea, and it is unfalsifiable with this data. We are giving up a 26% chance of being “right” about an item they would have bought anyway, for a 4.5% chance on one they rarely buy — on the theory that only the second is incremental. If the theory holds, the baseline’s 14% is mostly margin given away to people already walking to the cooler. If it does not, we are simply worse.

Nothing in a co-occurrence table can separate those two worlds. This is the same p₁ gap as everywhere else, and it is the sharpest argument for the holdout on this page: the pairing idea is the one place where we are deliberately choosing the lower-probability offer, so it is the place where being unable to measure uplift costs the most.

4 · A car pulls up

A card is dipped at the pump. It is read before the fuel flows, so identity arrives at the start of the fill, not the end. Everything here is known without touching a database:

shopper a37306b69b70… — hash of card BIN + last 4 + store state
clock 2026-06-15 07:12 → daypart morning
grade REGULAR
dollars pumped $38.82, live off the meter
store 8228

These are the gas features. Seven columns, no history — enough on their own to score a customer we have never seen.

5 · One lookup

A single read against a shopper-keyed table — precomputed nightly, not scanned live:

prior fuel visits 57
of those, went inside 52 (91%)
Category Bought before Share of prior visits
Packaged Beverages yes 86.0%
Cigarettes yes 36.8%
Candy yes 14.0%
Salty Snacks yes 7.0%
Other Tobacco / Nicotine no 0.0%
Beer no 0.0%

This is a regular, and a creature of habit: nine fills in ten end with a drink. Every figure is computed strictly from visits before this one — the window excludes the current row, which is the difference between a model that works and one that reads its own answer.

6 · Category Relevance (classification)

Six calibrated gradient-boosted classifiers, one per category, each scoring this fill directly:

Category P(buys it on this fill) Offerable?
Packaged Beverages 0.6782 yes
Cigarettes 0.3613 no — regulated
Salty Snacks 0.2727 yes
Candy 0.0852 yes
Other Tobacco / Nicotine 0.0020 no — regulated
Beer 0.0004 yes

There is no separate “will they come inside” model. These are unconditional — coming inside is already inside them. An earlier version multiplied an attach model in front; measured against scoring directly, the two matched to three decimals, so the stage was deleted.

What drives this shopper: context, plus their own record — inside on 98% of 55 prior fills, with their category mix in the features. The single strongest feature is the prior attach rate: how many times we have seen someone is worth +0.004 AUC, what share of those visits ended inside is worth +0.109.

If you want P(enters the store) as a number, derive it:

P(enters) = 1 − Π(1 − pᵢ) = 0.78

AUC 0.822 against a dedicated attach model’s 0.827. Report it — but keep it out of the expected value, or the category term counts twice.

Read saturated scores carefully. Isotonic calibration is a step function, so an emphatic history can push a category to the top or bottom step — 1.0 or 0.0 exactly. Both are clamped, but a score at either rail means “as confident as the calibrator can express”, not a certainty.

7 · SKU Affinity (ALS)

Stage one has already settled the category

The six probabilities above pick it: the most likely category we are allowed to show. For this shopper that is Packaged Beverages at 0.6782. Stage two may only look inside it.

That restriction is deliberate. An earlier version ranked the whole catalog in one pass by expected value, and offered candy to a customer the model was 90.6% sure wanted a beverage. Not a coding bug — a denominator. p_sku is normalised within its category, so a four-SKU category concentrates its probability into four numbers while beverages spread theirs across hundreds. A 2% category with a short tail out-earns a 90% category with a long one, and the two expected values were never comparable in the first place.

So: relevance picks the category, economics picks the item inside it. Margin cannot overrule relevance. Ties in stage one — a brand-new customer is at the probability floor in all six — go to the daypart’s best-selling category, because otherwise the alphabet decides and “Beer” wins.

This shopper is in the ALS factor matrix, so the ranking is personal rather than a best-seller list. That puts them in the minority: 31.6% of scored fills have enough category history to qualify. The other 68.4% get the Best-Seller Fallback, which is the primary path, not a degraded one.

Where ALS does run it earns the slot — hit@10 of 34.3% against the best seller’s 16.9% on the same fills, a 2.0× lift. See validate_sku.py.

Brand P(sku) Margin Brand funds Our cost
Red Bull 0.0055 $2.48 100% $0.00
Red Bull 0.0027 $4.44 100% $0.00
Dasani 0.0041 $3.68 0% $0.90
Red Bull 0.0043 $2.03 100% $0.00

P(sku) is normalised within the category — “given they buy a drink, which drink” — which is what the decision multiplies against P(category). Ranked on p_buys, not on expected value. Inside one category a fat margin on a less likely item would otherwise beat a thin margin on a likely one — and those margins are average price × an assumed 40%, so that ordering would be an assumption choosing the offer. P(category) is constant down this table, so it cannot change the order; it only converts P(sku) into a probability you can multiply by dollars later.

8 · Offer Economics

The arithmetic

  P(category)      0.6782
x P(sku)           0.0055
  ------------------------
  P(buys this)     0.003708
x effective margin $2.48 - $0.00 = $2.48
  ------------------------
  expected value   $0.00920 per fill

Show: Red Bull.

Dasani carries a bigger margin at $3.68 and still loses: it is self-funded, so $0.90 of that is ours to give away, while Red Bull’s discount is covered in full by the brand.

A tenth of a cent sounds like nothing. Across 36.1M fuel visits it is not — and more to the point, it is positive, which a self-funded version of the same offer would not be.

These dollars do not leave this page. unit_margin is average price times an assumed 40% — the PDI extract has no cost column — so the arithmetic above is a worked demonstration of the rule, not a measurement. The scored table (customer_scores.parquet) carries probabilities only: affinity, p_sku, p_category, p_buys. The margin terms live in negotiate.py, where the assumption is the subject rather than a hidden input.

9 · The pairing offer — the second item

Everything above answers what will they buy. That makes it the wrong thing to discount. A promotion on the item they had already decided to buy gives away margin and changes no behaviour; it is the one offer guaranteed to lose.

So the useful question is the second one. 46.5% of drink baskets are drink-only. That is the addressable population: a customer who is going to buy a drink regardless, and might be persuaded to add something.

Three numbers, and all three are needed

What it is What it is for
basket_lift P(B given A in basket) over P(B) Basket co-occurrence. Symmetric
cohort_index B’s attach rate among A-regulars ÷ the rate among equally frequent other shoppers Gating
excess the same comparison as a difference, in attach points Ranking
headroom 1 − this shopper’s own rate of B Don’t discount what they already buy

Controlling exposure is the whole trick. Every cohort here is defined as “at least five purchase baskets”, so every cohort is a cohort of heavy shoppers and indexes high on nearly everything. The first cut of this analysis had drink regulars over-indexing on frozen food at 6.1×, which was pure visit frequency. Comparing against shoppers who buy just as often and do not buy the anchor divides that out.

Drink regulars vs equally frequent others

Pair category Their rate Others Index Excess
Alternative Snacks 0.0602 0.0346 1.74 2.6pp
Commissary 0.0316 0.0193 1.64 1.2pp
Packaged Sweet Snacks 0.0669 0.0474 1.41 2.0pp
Salty Snacks 0.1218 0.0938 1.30 2.8pp
Candy 0.1217 0.1142 1.07 0.8pp
Cigarettes 0.0995 0.2258 0.44 —
Beer 0.0373 0.1893 0.20 —

Gate on the index, rank on the excess. The failure modes are opposite. Volume alone picks the popular item — Candy is 18.2% of every second item in a drink basket and looks obvious, but at an index of 1.07 drink regulars buy it barely more than anyone else; it is not a complement, it is just everywhere. The index alone picks the tiny item — Other Dairy indexes 1.32 on a 0.55% attach rate. The excess is in attach points, so it ranks by how much extra business the pairing is associated with, and it puts candy fifth.

There are two missions, and they repel each other

Anchor Best complement Verdict
Packaged Beverages Alternative Snacks 1.74 Pairs well
Salty Snacks Alternative Snacks 1.97, Candy 1.56 Pairs well
Beer Liquor 1.08 — nothing else above 0.63 Never paired (by rule)
Cigarettes General Merchandise 2.46 — nothing else above 0.65 Do not pair

The snack-and-drink categories are mutually complementary. The beer/tobacco/lottery categories are complementary with each other and strongly negative against the whole first group. A beer run is not a snack run. For a beer anchor the honest output is no pair offer at all — every candidate is something beer regulars buy less than average.

Alcohol is out of the pairing question entirely

Beer and cigarettes sit at the bottom of that table for the same reason, but only one of them is a rule. config.NOT_PAIRABLE excludes Beer, Wine and Liquor on both sides: a beer anchor returns no pair offer, and alcohol is never offered as a second item. The measurement happens to agree — beer’s only complement above 1.0 is Liquor at 1.08 — but the rule does not depend on that holding, and a pump screen answering “beer” with “and a bottle of liquor” is not a merchandising idea.

This is narrower than NOT_OFFERABLE. Beer remains a legitimate anchor offer; it simply neither takes nor becomes a second item.

The catalog had to grow to answer this

The three strongest complements to a drink were not in offer_catalog.csv at all, because TARGET_CATEGORIES was chosen for the anchor question and these are categories almost nobody buys anyway. The engine could name a category and then shrug. So SKU_MODEL_CATEGORIES is now TARGET_CATEGORIES + PAIR_CATEGORIES:

Added category SKUs in catalog Index vs a drink
Alternative Snacks 468 1.74
Packaged Sweet Snacks 251 1.41
Commissary & Other Packaged 135 1.64

Safe for the anchor path by construction: stage one only considers categories that have a Category Relevance model, and those are fitted over TARGET_CATEGORIES alone. The new categories reach the catalog and the ALS factors and nothing else — they can be the second item, never the first.

It ships as a column

customer_scores.parquet carries second_category and second_category_skus beside offer_category and top_skus — populated on 560,690 of 583,583 rows, empty where nothing clears the gate.

The struct deliberately has no p_category and no p_buys. The pair category usually sits outside the six, so it has no relevance model and there is no P(category) to multiply; a product built from a placeholder would read as a measurement. It carries cohort_index, excess and headroom instead — the gate, the rank, and the room left to grow.

None of this is causal, and this is the same gap as everywhere else. Co-occurrence says these things appear together; it does not say a discount on B causes a B purchase. That needs a holdout, and there is not one.

Two gaps this page cannot close

No logged propensity. A production version must write down what it showed and the probability it showed it with. Without that, off-policy evaluation is impossible later and the information cannot be reconstructed after the fact — it is the cheapest thing on this list and the easiest to forget.

Assumed margins. unit_margin is average price × 40%. The extract has no cost column, so every dollar figure here moves the moment someone supplies real ones. The probabilities do not.

Run it yourself

cd data/pipeline_analysis
UV="uv run --with polars --with scikit-learn --with joblib --with implicit"

$UV python explain.py --demo
$UV python explain.py --shopper <id> --time "2026-06-15 17:45" --grade DIESEL
$UV python explain.py --demo --json          # the same trace as a dict

serve.py gives the answer; explain.py gives the reasoning; negotiate.py gives the funding floor; score_table.py writes the same answer for every fill in the held-out period. All four read the same artifacts, and all four rank through the single function in scoring.py — serving is just the one-row case. That matters more than it sounds: when the live path and the batch path each had their own copy of the ranking, the copies drifted twice, and both times the table on disk recommended beer to customers whose beer propensity was 0.004. Three of the 68 checks in validate.py now score the same twelve fills both ways and require the probabilities, the expected values and the chosen SKU to match exactly.
